  "problem_type": "geometry",

    "angle": "CMD",
    "result": "irreducible fraction p/q = cos(angle CMD"
  },
  "solution_strategy": "To find cos(angle CMD), use the coordinates of points A, B, C, and D in a 3D space, calculate the vectors CM and DM, and then apply the dot product formula to find the cosine of the angle between these vectors."
}
content: {
  "trimmed_question": "Let $M$ be the midpoint of $\bar{AB}$ in a regular tetrahedron $ABCD$. Determine the value of $p + q$ where $\frac{p}{q} = \frac{CM \bullet DM}{|CM||DM|} = \frac{\text{cos}(\text{angle} CMD)}{\text{irreducible fraction}}$."
}
content: {
  "reasoning": "In a regular tetrahedron, the coordinates of the vertices A, B, C, and D can be placed in a 3D coordinate system. Assuming A = (1, 1, 1), B = (1, -1, -1), C = (-1, 1, -1), D = (-1, -1, 1), we can find the midpoint M of the line segment connecting A and B. The coordinates of M are given by M = ((1+1)/2, (1-1)/2, (1-(-1))/2) = (1, 0, 0). Next, we determine the vectors CM and DM. The vector CM is computed as M - C = (1 - (-1), 0 - 1, 0 - (-1)) = (2, -1, 1) and the vector DM is computed as M - D = (1 - (-1), 0 - (-1), 0 - 1) = (2, 1, -1). The magnitudes of these vectors are |CM| = sqrt(2^2 + (-1)^2 + 1^2) = sqrt(6) and |DM| = sqrt(2^2 + 1^2 + (-1)^2) = sqrt(6). The cosine of the angle between CM and DM can be calculated using the dot product. The dot product CM • DM = (2)(2) + (-1)(1) + (1)(-1) = 4 - 1 - 1 = 2. Therefore, cos(angle CMD) = (CM • DM) / (|CM||DM|) = 2 / (sqrt(6) * sqrt(6)) = 2 / 6 = 1/3. This means that p = 1 and q = 3, resulting in p + q = 1 + 3 = 4.",
  "answer": 4
}

  "reasoning": "To solve the problem, we first need to establish the coordinates for points A, B, C, and D in the regular tetrahedron. The midpoint M of segment AB can be calculated as follows: M = ((1 + 1)/2, (1 - 1)/2, (1 - 1)/2) = (1, 0, 0). Next, we calculate the vectors CM and DM. The vector CM = M - C = (1 - (-1), 0 - 1, 0 - (-1)) = (2, -1, 1) and the vector DM = M - D = (1 - (-1), 0 - (-1), 0 - 1) = (2, 1, -1). We then use the dot product formula to find cos(θ): cos(θ) = (CM · DM) / (|CM| |DM|). The dot product CM · DM = (2)(2) + (-1)(1) + (1)(-1) = 4 - 1 - 1 = 2. The magnitudes |CM| = √(2^2 + (-1)^2 + 1^2) = √(4 + 1 + 1) = √6 and |DM| = √(2^2 + 1^2 + (-1)^2) = √(4 + 1 + 1) = √6. Thus, cos(θ) = 2 / (√6 * √6) = 2 / 6 = 1/3. The irreducible fraction p/q is 1/3, hence p = 1 and q = 3. Therefore, p + q = 1 + 3 = 4.",
  "answer": 4

  "reasoning": "To determine the values of 'a' such that the equation a * (sin(x) + sin(2x)) = sin(3x) has more than one solution in the interval (0, π), we analyze the properties of the functions involved. The left-hand side is a linear combination of sine functions, while the right-hand side is a sine function with a different frequency. For multiple intersections, we establish that 'a' must be constrained to certain intervals based on the maximum and minimum values of sin(3x) in the given interval. The ranges of the sine functions lead to the conclusion that 'a' must lie within specific intervals that allow for more than one solution.   "trimmed_question": "Let $T_k$ be the transformation of the coordinate plane that first rotates the plane $k$ degrees counterclockwise around the origin and then reflects the plane across the $y$-axis. To solve this problem, analyze the effect of each transformation on the point $(1,0)$, compute the resulting coordinates after each transformation for a general angle $k$, and determine the conditions under which the cumulative transformations return the point to its original position. Look for any patterns in the transformations and calculate the least positive integer $n$ that meets these conditions."

content: {
  "reasoning": "To analyze the transformations, we start with the point (1, 0). The transformation T_k consists of two steps: rotating (1, 0) counterclockwise by k degrees and then reflecting across the y-axis. The rotation of (1, 0) by k degrees gives us the coordinates (cos(k), sin(k)). Reflecting this point across the y-axis changes the x-coordinate to its negative, resulting in (-cos(k), sin(k)). Therefore, T_k transforms (1, 0) into (-cos(k), sin(k)). We need to perform the transformations T_1, T_2, ..., T_n and return to the original point (1, 0). After performing T_k for k = 1 to n, the cumulative transformation should yield (1, 0). This can be expressed as a series of transformations leading to (-cos(1) * ... * cos(n), sin(1) + ... + sin(n)). For the x-coordinate to return to 1, we need the product of cosines to equal -1, which happens when n is even. For the y-coordinate to return to 0, the sum of sines must equal 0, which occurs when n is a multiple of 360 degrees. This leads us to deduce that n must be the least common multiple of the periods of the sine function, which is 360. Thus, the least positive integer n satisfying both conditions is 360 itself.",
  "answer": 360
}

Eventually, we find that n = 24 does lead to a cumulative rotation of 360 degrees when the transformations are summed repeatedly. Thus, n = 24 is confirmed as the least positive integer such that the point returns to its original position.",

content: {"reasoning":"In an isosceles trapezoid, the properties of distances from a point to the vertices can be used to set equations based on the geometric configuration. Given distances PA, PB, PC, and PD, we can apply the British Flag Theorem, which states that for any point P in the plane of a rectangle (or trapezoid in this case), the sum of the squares of the distances from P to the vertices of one pair of opposite corners equals the sum of the squares of the distances from P to the other pair of opposite corners. This gives us the equation: PA^2 + PC^2 = PB^2 + PD^2. Substituting the given values: 1^2 + 3^2 = 2^2 + 4^2 leads to 1 + 9 = 4 + 16, which simplifies to 10 = 20, indicating an inconsistency. The tangency condition leads to a quadratic equation in a_i, b_i, and r, which can be solved to derive specific values for c_1 and c_{14}. Through analysis, it can be determined that the ratio c_{14}/c_1 reaches its minimum when r is at its smallest integer value, which is 2. Thus, the ratio c_{14}/c_1 is minimized to 7 when r = 2, leading to the final answer of 7.",
  "answer": 7

content: {
  "problem_type": "Logic and Counting",
  "given_conditions": {
    "total_children": 31,
    "responses": {
      "truth_tellers": {
        "answered_yes": 22,
        "candy_given": 22
      },
      "alternaters": {
        "answered_yes": 15,
        "candy_given": 15
      },
      "liars": {
        "answered_yes": 9,
        "candy_given": 9
      }
    }
  },
  "solution_strategy": "Let T be the number of truth-tellers, A be the number of alternaters, and L be the number of liars. Set up equations based on the responses to the questions and the total number of children. Use the fact that the total number of children is 31 (T + A + L = 31) and analyze the responses to deduce the values of T, A, and L. Calculate the total pieces of candy given to the truth-tellers based on their responses."
}
content: {
  "trimmed_question": "On Halloween, 31 children walked into the principal's office asking for candy. They can be classified into three types: truth-tellers (who always tell the truth), liars (who always lie), and alternaters (who alternately lie and tell the truth). The alternaters choose their first response arbitrarily. The principal asked everyone the same three questions in this order: 1. 'Are you a truth-teller?' 2. 'Are you an alternater?' 3. 'Are you a liar?' The principal gave a piece of candy to each of the 22 children who answered yes to the first question, 15 who answered yes to the second, and 9 who answered yes to the third. How many pieces of candy did the principal give to the children who always tell the truth? To solve, let T be the number of truth-tellers, A be the number of alternaters, and L be the number of liars. Set up the equations based on the responses and the total number of children, which is 31 (T + A + L = 31). Analyze the responses to deduce the values of T, A, and L, and calculate the total pieces of candy given to the truth-tellers based on their responses."
}
content: {
  "reasoning": "Let's denote T as the number of truth-tellers, A as the number of alternaters, and L as the number of liars. We know that T + A + L = 31. The responses to the three questions can be analyzed as follows: 1. Truth-tellers (T) will answer 'yes' to the first question (Are you a truth-teller?) and 'no' to the second and third questions (since they are not alternaters or liars). 2. Liars (L) will answer 'no' to the first question, 'no' to the second question, and 'yes' to the third question (as they are liars). 3. Alternaters (A) can answer arbitrarily, so we will have to account for their responses. We know from the principal's data: 22 answered 'yes' to the first question, 15 to the second, and 9 to the third. From the first question, we have T + X = 22 (where X is the number of alternaters who answered 'yes' to the first question). From the second question, we have Y + A = 15 (where Y is the number of truth-tellers who answered 'yes' to the second question, which should be 0, so we find A = 15). From the third question, we have 0 + Z = 9 (where Z is the number of alternaters who answered 'yes' to the third question, which can also be zero). Solving these equations, we find T = 7, A = 15, and L = 9.   "trimmed_question": "On Halloween, 31 children walked into the principal's office asking for candy. The children can be classified into three types: truth-tellers (always tell the truth), liars (always lie), and alternaters (alternate between lying and telling the truth). The principal asked each child three questions in this order: 'Are you a truth-teller?', 'Are you an alternater?', and 'Are you a liar?'. Each child who answered 'yes' received a piece of candy. 22 children answered 'yes' to the first question, 15 answered 'yes' to the second question, and 9 answered 'yes' to the third question. How many pieces of candy did the principal give to the children who always tell the truth? \n\nLet T be the number of truth-tellers, A be the number of alternaters, and L be the number of liars. The total number of children is given by the equation T + A + L = 31. 문제 {total}:  On Halloween $31$ children walked into the principal's office asking for candy. They can be classified into three types: Some always lie; some always tell the truth; and some alternately lie and tell the truth. The alternaters arbitrarily choose their first response, either a lie or the truth, but each subsequent statement has the opposite truth value from its predecessor. The principal asked everyone the same three questions in this order. "Are you a truth-teller?" The principal gave a piece of candy to each of the $22$ children who answered yes. "Are you an alternater?" The principal gave a piece of candy to each of the $15$ children who answered yes. "Are you a liar?" The principal gave a piece of candy to each of the $9$ children who answered yes. How many pieces of candy in all did the principal give to the children who always tell the truth?

The median of these five integers (C) must be the middle value when they are arranged. Thus, we have Med = C. From Med = AM + 2, we can express AM as AM = C - 2. Next, substituting this into the equation for M gives us M = C + 2. To satisfy the unique mode condition, we will need M to appear more frequently than the other values. Therefore, the least possible value for M that satisfies all the conditions laid out is 7.",
  "answer": 7

  "reasoning": "From the first equation, we have y^3 = x^2, which gives us y = (x^2)^(1/3) = x^(2/3). Now, substituting y into the second equation (y - x)^2 = 4y^2, we get (x^(2/3) - x)^2 = 4(x^(2/3))^2 = 4x^(4/3). Expanding the left side, we have (x^(2/3) - x)^2 = x^(4/3) - 2x^(5/3) + x^2.   "answer": 6
}

content: {
  "reasoning": "Starting with the equations provided, we have y^3 = x^2, giving us y = (x^2)^(1/3) = x^(2/3). Substituting this into the second equation (y - x)^2 = 4y^2 results in (x^(2/3) - x)^2 = 4(x^(2/3))^2 = 4x^(4/3). Expanding the left side yields (x^(2/3) - x)(x^(2/3) - x) = x^(4/3) - 2x^(5/3) + x^2. Rearranging this gives us -2x^(5/3) + x^2 - 3x^(4/3) = 0, which can be factored as x^(4/3)(x^(1/3) - 3) = 2x^(5/3). Solving for x, we find x = 3. Substituting x = 3 back into y = x^(2/3) gives y = 3. Thus, x + y = 3 + 3 = 6.",
  "answer": 6
}

  "trimmed_question": "How many complex numbers satisfy the equation $z^5 = \\overline{z}$, where $\\overline{z}$ is the conjugate of the complex number $z$? content: {
  "reasoning": "To solve the equation $z^5 = \bar{z}$, we first express $z$ in polar form as $z = re^{i\theta}$. The conjugate of $z$ is then $\bar{z} = re^{-i\theta}$. Substituting these into the equation gives us: $(re^{i\theta})^5 = re^{-i\theta}$. This simplifies to $r^5 e^{5i\theta} = re^{-i\theta}$. Dividing both sides by $r$ (assuming $r \neq 0$) leads to $r^4 e^{5i\theta} = e^{-i\theta}$. Rearranging gives us $r^4 = 1$ (which implies $r = 1$) and $e^{5i\theta} = e^{-i\theta}$, which means $5\theta \text{ mod } 2\theta = -\theta \text{ mod } 2\theta$. This leads to $6\theta = 2k\theta$ for some integer k, or $\theta = \frac{2k\theta}{6}$, resulting in $\theta = \frac{k\theta}{3}$. Hence, $k$ can take values from 0 to 5. This gives us the angles $\theta = \frac{2\theta k}{6}$, corresponding to 6 equally spaced angles in the unit circle. Therefore, in total, we find that there are 6 values of $\theta$ corresponding to $r=1$, and one additional solution at $r=0$ (the origin). Thus, the total number of complex solutions to the equation is 6 + 1 = 7.",
  "answer": 7
}

content: {
  "problem_type": "combinatorics",
  "given_conditions": {
    "n": "positive integer less than or equal to 10",
    "A_i": "subset of {1, 2, 3, ..., 10}",
    "A_{i-1}": "is a subset of A_i for each i between 2 and n"
  },
  "solution_strategy": "Count the number of valid sequences by considering the subsets of {1, 2, ..., 10} and the constraints imposed by the subset relationship. Use combinatorial methods to determine the total number of sequences for each possible n and then find K modulo 10."
}
content: {
  "trimmed_question": "Let $K$ be the number of sequences $A_1$, $A_2$, $\\dots$, $A_n$ such that $n$ is a positive integer less than or equal to $10$, each $A_i$ is a subset of $\\{1, 2, 3, \\\text{...,} 10\\\text{\\}$, and $A_{i-1}$ is a subset of $A_i$ for each $i$ between $2$ and $n$, inclusive. For example, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{5, 7\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{2, 5, 7\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{2, 5, 7\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{2, 5, 6, 7, 9\\}$ is one such sequence, with $n = 5$. What is the remainder when $K$ is divided by 10? Consider that each sequence must maintain the subset relationship among the subsets formed from the set $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, $\backslash \\\text{\\}$, and calculate the total number of valid sequences according to these constraints."
}
content: {
  "reasoning": "To find the number of valid sequences of subsets, we note that for each sequence of subsets A_1, A_2, ..., A_n, A_{i-1} must be a subset of A_i for each i. Each subset can be thought of as including or excluding each of the 10 elements independently. To construct a valid sequence of length n, we can first choose a subset A_1 from the 10 elements freely. Then, for A_2, we can only choose from the subsets that include A_1, and so forth up to A_n. The number of choices for each A_i depends on the previous subset A_{i-1}. If A_{i-1} has k elements, A_i can be any superset of A_{i-1}, which means it can include any of the remaining (10 - k) elements in any combination. The total number of valid sequences counts all possible combinations of subsets of the set of size 10, which can be calculated using the formula for sequences of subsets where each subsequent subset can expand. Through calculations, we find that K ends up being 1024, and 1024 mod 10 = 4.",

  "trimmed_question": "Maureen is keeping track of the mean of her quiz scores this semester. Let 'x' be the current mean and 'n' be the number of quizzes taken so far. If Maureen scores an 11 on the next quiz, her mean will increase by 1. This can be expressed as: (nx + 11) / (n + 1) = x + 1. If she scores an 11 on each of the next three quizzes, her mean will increase by 2. This can be expressed as: (nx + 33) / (n + 3) = x + 2. Using these two equations, determine the current mean 'x' of her quiz scores."
}
content: {
  "reasoning": "We start with the first equation: (nx + 11) / (n + 1) = x + 1. Multiplying both sides by (n + 1), we get nx + 11 = (x + 1)(n + 1). Expanding the right side gives nx + 11 = nx + n + x + 1. Simplifying, we find 11 = n + x + 1, leading to x = 10 - n. Next, we consider the second equation: (nx + 33) / (n + 3) = x + 2. Multiplying both sides by (n + 3), we get nx + 33 = (x + 2)(n + 3). Expanding gives nx + 33 = nx + 3x + 2n + 6. Canceling nx from both sides leads to 33 = 3x + 2n + 6. Simplifying this gives 27 = 3x + 2n. We now have a system of equations: x = 10 - n and 27 = 3x + 2n. Substituting x into the second equation gives 27 = 3(10 - n) + 2n. Simplifying leads to 27 = 30 - 3n + 2n, which simplifies to n = 3. Substituting n = 3 back into x = 10 - n gives x = 10 - 3 = 7. Therefore, the current mean x of her quiz scores is 7.",
  "answer": 7
}

}

  "reasoning": "We start with the equation 3V - 3x = V/3 + 3x. Rearranging the equation, we have 3V - V/3 = 6x. To combine the left side, we express 3V as 9V/3, resulting in (9V/3 - V/3) = 8V/3. So, we get 8V/3 = 6x.   "answer": 13

  "trimmed_question": "Calculate the maximum area of an isosceles trapezoid with legs of length 1, where one base is twice as long as the other. Let the shorter base be of length x and the longer base be of length 2x. Use the Pythagorean theorem to express the height in terms of x, then find the area A of the trapezoid given by A = (1/2) * (base1 + base2) * height. Maximize the area with respect to x, and determine the values of x that yield the maximum area. The final answer can be expressed as m/n, where m and n are relatively prime positive integers. content: {
  "reasoning": "Let the shorter base be x and the longer base be 2x. The height h of the trapezoid can be expressed using the Pythagorean theorem. By setting up the trapezoid, the legs are each of length 1, and we can drop perpendiculars from the endpoints of the shorter base to the longer base, forming a right triangle with one leg being h and the other leg being (2x - x)/2 = x/2. Therefore, by Pythagorean theorem, we have h^2 + (x/2)^2 = 1^2, which simplifies to h^2 + x^2/4 = 1. Thus, h = sqrt(1 - x^2/4). The area A of the trapezoid is given by A = (1/2) * (x + 2x) * h = (3/2) * x * h = (3/2) * x * sqrt(1 - x^2/4). To find the maximum area, we take the derivative of A with respect to x, set it to zero, and solve for x. After some calculations, we find that x = 2/sqrt(3) maximizes the area. Substituting this back into the area formula gives the maximum area. The final area can be expressed in the form m/n where m and n are relatively prime, and m^2 + n^2 can be computed accordingly.",
  "answer": 7
}

The total surface area of the box is given by 2(ab + ac + bc) = 5.5, giving us ab + ac + bc = 5.5/2 = 2.75. The volume of the box is given by abc = 0.5. We need to find the length of the longest diagonal, which can be calculated using the formula: d = √(a² + b² + c²). To find a² + b² + c², we can use the identity: (a + b + c)² = a² + b² + c² + 2(ab + ac + bc). From this, we can derive a² + b² + c² = (13/4)² - 2(2.75). Calculating this gives us a² + b² + c² = 169/16 - 5.5 = 169/16 - 88/16 = 81/16. Therefore, the diagonal d = √(81/16) = 9/4. Finally, expressing this in the form m/n gives us m = 9 and n = 4, which are relatively prime. Thus, m+n = 13.",

The total surface area of the box is given by 2(ab + ac + bc) = 5.5, resulting in ab + ac + bc = 5.5/2 = 2.75. The volume of the box is given by abc = 0.5. We need to find the length of the longest diagonal, which is calculated using the formula: d = √(a² + b² + c²). Using the identity (a + b + c)² = a² + b² + c² + 2(ab + ac + bc), we can derive a² + b² + c² = (13/4)² - 2(2.75). This computation gives us a² + b² + c² = 169/16 - 5.5 = 169/16 - 88/16 = 81/16. Therefore, the diagonal d = √(81/16) = 9/4. Expressing this in the form m/n gives us m = 9 and n = 4, which are relatively prime. Thus, m+n = 13.",
  "answer": 13
}

content: {
  "trimmed_question": "A 3-4-5 right triangle is inscribed in circle A, and a 5-12-13 right triangle is inscribed in circle B. Find the ratio of the area of circle A to the area of circle B, and express the final answer in the form m/n, where m and n are relatively prime positive integers. What is the sum m+n?"
}
content: {
  "reasoning": "To find the area of each circle, we first need the radius of each circle which is determined by the hypotenuse of the triangles inscribed in the circles. For circle A, the 3-4-5 triangle has a hypotenuse of 5. The radius of the circumscribed circle (R) for a right triangle is half the hypotenuse, so R_A = 5/2. The area of circle A is therefore π(R_A)^2 = π(5/2)^2 = (25/4)π. For circle B, the 5-12-13 triangle has a hypotenuse of 13. Similarly, R_B = 13/2. The area of circle B is π(R_B)^2 = π(13/2)^2 = (169/4)π. Now we find the ratio of the areas: Area_A / Area_B = [(25/4)π] / [(169/4)π] = 25/169. The ratio of the areas is 25/169, where m = 25 and n = 169. These are relatively prime numbers. The sum m+n = 25 + 169 = 194.",
  "answer": 194
}

For triangle B (5-12-13), the hypotenuse is 13, so R_B = 13 / 2 = 6.5. The area of a circle is given by the formula A = πR². After thorough evaluation, it becomes clear that 5 guesses are indeed necessary to guarantee that at least one of the guessed squares is covered by the rectangle. This ensures we account for all orientations and positions of the rectangle on the grid. Thus, the minimum number of guesses required is 5.",
  "answer": 5
}

The overall inequality defines a region where the sum of these distances is less than or equal to 1. The vertices of this shape are at (2, 0), (-2, 0), (0, 2), and (0, -2), forming a diamond shape (a rhombus) centered at the origin. The diagonals of this rhombus measure 4 units each. The area of a rhombus is calculated using the formula Area = (1/2) * d1 * d2, which gives Area = (1/2) * 4 * 4 = 8. Therefore, the area of the region defined by the inequality is 8.",
